Note that balance is implied by either the static or strong condition, and that copyable idempotents are all three (and conversely)
[
FGL+23
, Remark 4.1.2]
.
39
39

39

The cited source does this for Markov categories, but the arguments only depend on the CD structure and thus still hold.

3
Partializable Markov categories

3.1
Partial morphisms in categorical probability

Restriction categories are a modern framework that has shown to be useful for the study of partial maps in a variety of contexts.
Recent work suggests quasi-Markov categories as a suitable framework for partial maps in categorical probability, as they are CD categories with a notion of domain inducing a ‘‘restriction partial order’’.
40
40

40

To be precise, works like
[
LT23
,
GRSDlC24
]
have studied order-like relations on the hom-sets of a CD category, but only given quasi-totality do these truly define partial orders on the hom-sets. See
Remark

1.23
for a discussion.

We will in fact show that positive quasi-Markov categories are restriction categories, so that all the results of
[
CL02
]
apply (this also supplies another way to see the poset enrichment of
Proposition

2.12
).
41
41

41

However we warn that Cockett and Lack’s results on restriction products
[
CL07
]
do not necessarily apply a priori, as they restrict to the cartesian product on the subcategory of total maps.

Proposition 3.1

(Generalizing
[
CGT25b
, Corollary 4.12]
to a non-copyable setting)
.

Every positive quasi-Markov category is a restriction category, with the restriction
\bar{f}
associated to a map
f
given by the domain
\mathrm{dom}(f)
of
Definition

2.8
.

Proof.

We verify the four conditions
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{R.1}}}\right)
–
(\mathrm{R.}4)
of
Definition

2.3
:

(R.1):

For the proposed restriction structure, this is quasi-totality (
Definition

2.9
);

(R.2):

This is a consequence of the copy maps defining co-commutative co-monoids;

(R.3):

This is again a consequence of the copy maps defining co-monoids;

(R.4):

Consider a composable pair
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
.
A consequence of quasi-totality is that the composite
\discard\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f
is copyable.
Thus, by positivity (
Definition

2.11
), we have

f
f
g
=
f
g

This is the desired equation
f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\overline{g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f}=\bar{g}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f
.

∎

Remark 3.2
.

Cioffo et al. show in their recent
[
CGT25b
, Corollary 4.12]
that positive ‘‘strict oplax cartesian categories’’ are restriction categories.
These are poset
42
42

42

Oplax cartesian categories are defined to be preorder enriched, but they are termed strict when the preorders are posets.

enriched
CD categories such that copyability and totality hold up to inequality
[
CGT25b
, Definition 4.5]
.
It has been shown that strict oplax cartesian categories are all quasi-Markov
[
FGTC23
, Proposition 3.6]
, and thus
Proposition

3.1
generalizes their result to a non-oplax-copyable setting.

However, the categories we consider here will generally not be oplax cartesian.
Indeed, positive oplax cartesian categories are equivalent to cartesian restriction categories (restriction categories with restriction products)
[
CGT25b
, Remark 4.11]
, and thus the partializations we construct will not be instances of these (see also
Remark

1.24
).

Remark 3.3
.

The positivity hypothesis is necessary
43
43

43

Even in the absence of positivity a quasi-Markov category is still a “support category”
[
CGH12
, Section 2.1]
(the author would like to thank Jean-Simon Lemay for suggesting this). Positivity is only needed to derive the “R.
4
” axiom for restriction categories, whereas the “weak R.
4
” axiom for support categories holds for general CD categories.
here, as even for a common quasi-Markov category like
\mathsf{Rel}
the restriction partial order of
Definition

2.10
need not be compatible with pre-composition
44
44

44

Consider relations
S,T\colon\{u,v\}\to\{x,y\}
, where
S=\{(u,x)\}
while
T=\{(u,x),(v,y)\}
. Then
S
is the restriction of
T
to its domain
\{u\}
.
On the other hand, pre-composing with
R\colon\{a\}\to\{u,v\}
one gets two distinct total relations, but
SR
has range
x
while
TR
has full range.
(and thus cannot be induced by a restriction structure, as these are always poset enrichments
45
45

45

This also shows that positivity is required for
Proposition

2.12
.
).
This suggests some connection between positivity and restriction structures, but a precise characterization of when the restriction partial order is an enrichment remains open (of course, one can have enrichment without positivity, as holds trivially for non-positive Markov categories).

3.2
The Partialization of a Markov category

We now show how to add partial morphisms to a Markov category
{\mathsf{C}}
to obtain a new quasi-Markov category.
This main idea is as follows:
We form a new category
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
where the objects are the ones of
{\mathsf{C}}
, and the morphisms
X\to Y
are equivalence classes of spans of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where the map
i
is a particular kind of monomorphism.
We occasionally denote such a span by a triple
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)
or merely a pair
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,f}}\right)
whenever the objects are clear from context. This is a typical categorical construction of a category of partial maps in terms of spans.

The intuition is that
f
is only defined on
D
, which is included within
X
via
i
.
The domain of
f
is thus in general not the whole of
X
.
However, as our morphisms are intuitively partial
stochastic
maps, we insist upon the condition that the domain inclusions
D\xhookrightarrow{i}X
be
deterministic
monomorphisms.
46
46

46

So all the randomness is in the “action” map
X\xrightarrow{f}Y
.

In order for the construction to work, we require a couple of conditions on the original Markov category.

Definition 3.4
.

A Markov category
\mathsf{C}
is called
partializable
if:

(i)

It is positive;
47
47

47

In such a case, all isomorphisms are deterministic
[
Fri20
, Remark 11.28]
.

(ii)

Pullbacks of deterministic monomorphisms exist and are themselves deterministic;

(iii)

If
i\colon D\to X
is a deterministic monomorphism then every
i\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}
is a monomorphism.

Some useful facts about partializable Markov categories are given in
Appendix

A
.

The three conditions ensure that in a partializable Markov category, the deterministic monomorphisms form what Cockett and Lack call in
[
CL02
, Section 3.1]
a
stable system of monics
(as in
Definition

2.1
).
This is in essence, the condition for the construction of a category
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
with objects those of
\mathsf{C}
and morphisms equivalence classes of spans
X\xhookleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}Y
, with
i
a deterministic monomorphism (as in
2.2
).

Definition 3.5
.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}
be a partializable Markov category.
The category
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
, termed
partialization
of
\mathsf{C}
, is constructed as follows:

•

The objects are the objects of the original category
{\mathsf{C}}
;

•

The morphisms
X\to Y
are equivalence classes of spans of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
i
is a
deterministic
monomorphism and where two spans
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D^{\prime},i^{\prime},f^{\prime}}}\right)
are considered equivalent if and only if there is an isomorphism
D\to D^{\prime}
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

•

The composition of two morphisms represented by spans
X\xhookleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}Y
and
Y\xhookleftarrow{j}B\xrightarrow{g}Z
is obtained by forming the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

and is represented by the span
X\xhookleftarrow{i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}u}C\xrightarrow{g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}v}Z
.

There is a canonical inclusion of
\mathsf{C}
into
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
sending
X\xrightarrow{f}Y
to the (equivalence class) of the span
X=X\xrightarrow{f}Y
.

Example 3.6

(standard Borel spaces and Markov kernels)
.

Our main example will be the Markov category
\mathsf{BorelStoch}

[
Fri20
, Section 4]
of standard Borel spaces (recall
Example

2.5

(iv)
).
Fritz has shown in
[
Fri20
, Example 11.25]
that
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
is positive.
Since the deterministic morphisms in
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
are exactly the measurable maps, it follows that the deterministic monomorphisms are exactly the measurable injections.
It is a non-trivial fact that every measurable injection also preserves measurable sets in the forward direction, and is therefore a Borel isomorphism onto its image.
48
48

48

See Kechris’s
[
Kec95
, Corollary (15.2)]
for a source.

In other words, the deterministic subobjects of a standard Borel space
X
are exactly its measurable subsets.
Hence the morphisms of type
X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{BorelStoch}}}}\right)
are pairs
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,f}}\right)
where
S\subseteq X
is measurable and
f\colon S\to Y
is a measurable map.

As an immediate consequence, we have that for such a deterministic monomorphism
i
and object
A
, the tensor
i\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}
is again a deterministic monomorphism (it is again a measurable subset inclusion).

Finally, concerning the pullback condition, consider a deterministic subobject
T\in\Sigma_{Y}
and a Markov kernel
f\colon X\to Y
.
Our goal is to construct a pullback diagram in
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f|_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
S\in\Sigma_{X}
.

Note that a necessary condition for such a square to even commute is that
f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T\,|\,x}}\right)=1
for all
x\in S
.
Indeed for
x\in S
,
f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T\,|\,x}}\right)
is the probability that the image of
s
under the lower-left path in the diagram is in
T
.
If the diagram commutes, this must also be the probability under the upper-right path,
f|_{S}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T\,|\,x}}\right)
.
However as this is a Markov kernel to
T
, the probability is
1
.

Motivated by this, we define
49
49

49

Where
T^{c}\coloneqq Y\setminus T
is the complement of
T
.

S\coloneq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x\in X\ :\ f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T\,|\,x}}\right)=1}}\right\}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x\in X\ :\ f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T^{c}\,|\,x}}\right)=0}}\right\}

which is a measurable subset of
X
by the measurability of
f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T\,|\,{\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}}}\right)
.

We noted that in general, a Markov kernel
h\colon W\to X
factors through a deterministic subobject
S\subseteq X
if and only if
h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S\,|\,w}}\right)=1
for all
w\in W
.
This lets us establish the pullback property as follows.
Given
h\colon W\to X
, we assume that
f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h
factors through
T
, which means that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T\,|\,w}}\right)=\int_{x\in X}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T\,|\,x}}\right)\,h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx\,|\,w}}\right)=1\qquad\forall w\in W,

or (by normalization of probability) equivalently that

\int_{x\in X}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T^{c}\,|\,x}}\right)\,h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx\,|\,w}}\right)=0\qquad\forall w\in W.

An integral of a nonnegative function vanishes if and only if the integrand vanishes almost everywhere.
Taking complements again, we can therefore conclude

f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T\,|\,{\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}}}\right)=_{h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.49164pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.40974pt]{4.91673pt}{0.40974pt}}{\kern 0.49164pt}\,|\,w}}\right)\text{-a.s.}}1

By the definition of
S
, this is equivalent to
h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S\,|\,w}}\right)=1
, which means that
h
indeed factors through
S
(necessarily uniquely).
Thus, the pullback condition holds as well.

Remark 3.7
.

The same argument of
Example

3.6
shows that the full subcategory
\mathsf{FinStoch}

[
Fri20
, Example 2.5]
(recall
Example

2.5

(i)
) of finite (discrete) spaces is also partializable.
This can also be seen as a consequence of the fact that all distributions involved are discrete, which is developed more generally in
Example

3.9
.

Example 3.8

(Sets and multivalued functions)
.

The category
{\mathsf{SetMulti}}
of sets and multivalued maps (
Example

2.5

(ii)
) is partializable.
To see this, note first that it has been shown to be positive (for instance, as a consequence of having conditionals).
Furthermore, it is the Kleisli category of the
nonempty
power set monad on
{\mathsf{Set}}
, and additionally representable.
In particular, the deterministic monomorphisms are precisely the injective functions.
Thus, the deterministic monomorphisms are also closed under tensor.

It only remains to check the pullback condition.
For this, one need only observe that a multivalued function (total relation)
X\xrightarrow{f}Y
factors across a deterministic subobject
T\subseteq Y
if and only if each image set
f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\subseteq T
(for all
x\in X
).

Thus, if we simply define
S\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x\in X\ :\ f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\subseteq T}}\right\}\subseteq X
, then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f|_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback.
Indeed, if any multivalued map
W\xrightarrow{h}X
is such that
f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h
factors through
T
(necessarily uniquely), then for each
w\in W
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)=\bigcup_{x\in h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\subseteq T
, and thus
h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)\subseteq S
.
Thus,
h
would have to factor through
S
(necessarily uniquely).

Thus
{\mathsf{SetMulti}}
satisfies all three conditions for partializability.

Example 3.9

(Distributions valued in an entire zerosumfree semiring)
.

For a semiring
R
(we assume all semirings to be commutative), Coumans and Jacobs in
[
CJ13
, Section 5.1]
have constructed a monad of “
R
-valued finitely supported distributions” on the category
\mathsf{Set}
of sets and functions.
Recall (
Example

2.5

(iii)
) that as observed in
[
FGPR23
, Example 3.3]
, its Kleisli category
\mathsf{Kl}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{R}}}\right)
is a Markov category (recall
Example

2.5

(iii)
) whose objects can be thought of as the category of discrete measurable spaces and whose morphisms are kernels

f\colon X\to Y,\qquad x\mapsto\sum_{y\in Y}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y\,|\,x}}\right)\delta_{y}

such that
\mathrm{Supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}\,|\,x}}\right)}}\right)\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{y\in Y:f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y\,|\,x}}\right)\neq 0}}\right\}
is finite.

Fritz et al. show later in
[
FGPR23
, Proposition 3.6]
that when the semiring is entire, the category
\mathsf{Kl}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{R}}}\right)
is representable, and the deterministic morphisms are functions between sets.
In particular, the deterministic
monomorphisms
are (like with
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
in
Example

3.6
) the inclusions of subsets.

In
[
FGHL+23
, Proposition 2.12]
, it is further shown that for an entire (commutative) semiring
R
, the category
\mathsf{Kl}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{R}}}\right)
is positive if and only if
R
is zerosumfree.
In fact, we show that for an entire zerosumfree semiring
R
, the category
\mathsf{Kl}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{R}}}\right)
is a partializable Markov category.

The fact that deterministic monomorphisms are measurable injections implies that they are closed under tensoring with identities.
Thus it only remains to check the pullback condition
(ii)
of
Definition

3.4
.
For this, consider again the pullback of a subobject
T\subseteq Y
along a
f\colon X\to Y
.
We claim that the subset

S\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x\in X\ :\ \mathrm{Supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}\,|\,x}}\right)}}\right)\subseteq T}}\right\}

defines the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f|_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

For this, note that for a kernel
h\colon W\to X
to factor across a subset
A\subseteq X
, a necessary and sufficient condition is that
\mathrm{Supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}\,|\,w}}\right)}}\right)\subseteq A
for all
w\in W
.
Thus, if for such an
h
the composite
f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h
factors across
T
, we must have
\mathrm{Supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}\,|\,w}}\right)}}\right)\subseteq T
for all
w\in W
.

Recall that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y\,|\,w}}\right)=\sum_{x\in X}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y\,|\,x}}\right)h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x\,|\,w}}\right)

Thus if
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y\,|\,w}}\right)=0
, zerosumfreeness implies that

f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y\,|\,x}}\right)h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x\,|\,w}}\right)=0

for all
x\in X
.
Hence as
R
is entire, one of the two factors must be zero.
In particular when
x\in\mathrm{Supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}\,|\,w}}\right)}}\right)
, we must have
f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y\,|\,x}}\right)=0
.

Consider an
x\in\mathrm{Supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}\,|\,w}}\right)}}\right)
.
We have just seen that
\mathrm{Supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}\,|\,x}}\right)}}\right)\subseteq\mathrm{Supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}\,|\,w}}\right)}}\right)
, which is in turn a subset of
T
.
Thus
x\in S
by construction, and as
x\in\mathrm{Supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}\,|\,w}}\right)}}\right)
was arbitrary, we conclude that
h
factors through
S
(necessarily uniquely).
Thus all three conditions have been verified and we conclude that
\mathsf{Kl}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{R}}}\right)
is partializable.

Remark 3.10
.

A consequence of zerosumfreeness in
Example

3.9
is that for a kernel
f\colon X\to Y
and a subset
A\subseteq Y
, the condition
\mathrm{Supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}\,|\,x}}\right)}}\right)\subseteq A
is equivalent to
f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A^{c}\,|\,x}}\right)=0
for all
x\in X
, as in
Example

3.6
.
Indeed, the entire argument could be recast in terms of the kernels assigning the complement of a subobject probability
0
.

However, while (as with
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
) it might be tempting to restate the condition in terms of the subobject having probability
1
, this is not equivalent in general.
Indeed, for semirings such as the Booleans (sub-example
(iii)
of
Example

3.12
) the condition
f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A^{c}\,|\,x}}\right)=0
is the assertion that
A
contains
all
possible images of
x
, whereas
f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A\,|\,x}}\right)=1
merely requires
A
to contain
one
of them.
When
R
is for instance, cancellative, such a problem is not possible and the two conditions are in fact equivalent.

Remark 3.11

(Subcategories of finite spaces)
.

For a (commutative) semiring
R
, the full subcategory
\mathsf{FinKl}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{R}}}\right)
spanned by the finite sets is closed under monoidal tensor (as these represented by cartesian products of finite sets), and this subcategory also contains the CD structure data (monoidal coherence maps and comonoid structures).
Consequently, these are Markov categories as well.
50
50

50

However they are no longer Kleisli categories (nor representable) in general, as the space of (finite) distributions need not be finite.

Furthermore, the argument of
Example

3.9
shows that
\mathsf{FinKl}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{R}}}\right)
is partializable as well.
One need only note that they are positive as sub-Markov categories of positive ones.
The deterministic monomorphisms remain the finite measurable injections and are hence closed under tensor.
And finally, the pullback “
S
” in
Example

3.9
is constructed as a subset of
X
, so is finite if
X
is.

Example 3.12
.

Sub-examples of
Examples

3.9
and
3.11
for two familiar semirings are of particular common interest:

(i)

The category
{\mathsf{Dist}}
of discrete probability distributions (
Example

2.5

(iii)(a)
), which can be seen as the subcategory of the category of measurable spaces and Markov kernels consisting of the discrete spaces and discrete kernels (ones that assign to each point of the source a mixture of point distributions on the target).
This is the Kleisli category
\mathsf{Kl}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{R}}}\right)
with
R
the nonnegative reals
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and is thus partializable.

(ii)

The full subcategory of
{\mathsf{\mathsf{Dist}}}
spanned by the finite sets,
\mathsf{FinKl}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}}\right)
, is precisely
\mathsf{FinStoch}
.
This supplies another way of seeing that
\mathsf{FinStoch}
is partializable.

(iii)

The category
{\mathsf{SetFinMulti}}
(
Example

2.5

(iii)(b)
) is the wide subcategory of
\mathsf{SetMulti}
comprising the finitely multivalued maps.
51
51

51

In other words those such that every element of the domain is related to some element of the codomain, and only finitely many.

This is the Kleisli category
\mathsf{Kl}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{R}}}\right)
with
R
the Booleans
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\bot,\top}}\right\},\lor,\land}}\right)
,
52
52

52

The elements being false and true, with addition and multiplication being disjunction and conjunction respectively.
and is thus partializable as well.

(iv)

The full subcategory of
{\mathsf{SetFinMulti}}
spanned by the finite sets is precisely
\mathsf{FinSetMulti}
, the category of finite sets and multivalued maps and is thus partializable as well.

Warning 3.13
.

We have seen in
Examples

3.6
,
3.12
and
3.8
that
\mathsf{BorelStoch},{\mathsf{Dist}}
, and
{\mathsf{SetMulti}}
are partializable Markov categories.
They thus have partializations
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{BorelStoch}}}}\right)
,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{Dist}}}}}\right)
, and
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{SetMulti}}}}}\right)
as in
Definition

3.5
, which can intuitively be thought of as categories with morphisms partially defined Markov kernels, discrete partially defined kernels, and partially defined multivalued maps.

A partially defined multivalued map is nothing but a relation, since every relation defines a multivalued map on its domain.
Thus, one may suspect that the category
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{SetMulti}}}}}\right)
is merely the category of relations
\mathsf{Rel}
.
Similarly, notions of categories of “partially defined Markov kernels” have appeared in earlier work on CD categories, such as Di Lavore and Román’s categories
\mathsf{BorelStoch}_{\leq 1}
53
53

53

Appearing under that name at
[
DLR23
, Definition 2.15]
, although it is the Kleisli category of the “subprobability measure” monad
\mathbf{G}_{\leq 1}
which appeared as
\Pi
in Panangaden’s
[
Pan99
, Section 4]
(or more accurately, is the restriction to standard Borel spaces).
and
\mathsf{Kl}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbf{D}_{\leq 1}}}\right)}
(the Kleisli category of the finite
sub-distribution
monad
[
DLR23
, Definition 2.11]
).
54
54

54

\mathsf{BorelStoch}_{\leq 1}
has also appeared in Lorenz and Tull’s
[
LT23
]
as
{\mathsf{BorelProb}}
. In the later work
[
DLRS
]
,
\mathsf{Kl}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbf{D}_{\leq 1}}}\right)}
is referred to as
{\mathsf{subStoch}}
.

Both
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
and
\mathsf{Dist}
are Kleisli categories for some kind of distribution monad
D
, and it can even be shown that the morphisms
X\rightharpoonup Y
of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{BorelStoch}}}}\right)
and
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{Dist}}}}}\right)
are precisely the Kleisli morphisms
X\to D\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y\sqcup\ast}}\right)
that factor across
DY\sqcup\ast
(with the domain being the subobject that maps into
DY
).
55
55

55

In other words, the image of each
x\in X
is either a probability measure or zero/undefined.

In light of these observations, the reader may well suspect that
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{BorelStoch}}}}\right)
,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{Dist}}}}}\right)
, and
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{SetMulti}}}}}\right)
are at the very least subcategories of
\mathsf{BorelStoch}_{\leq 1}
,
\mathsf{Kl}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbf{D}_{\leq 1}}}\right)}
, and
\mathsf{Rel}
under the aforementioned intuitive identifications of morphisms.
However, this is not the case as the composition operations differ.

For instance, consider a fair coin flip, composed with the partially defined (copyable!) kernel that assigns a value
a
to heads.
This can be seen as defining a composable sequence
\ast\xrightarrow{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{H,T}}\right\}\xrightarrow{f}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{a}}\right\}
.
As morphisms of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{BorelStoch}}}}\right)
or
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{Dist}}}}}\right)
, these are represented by
p
assigning probability
\frac{1}{2}
to both heads and tails, and
f
having domain of definition
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{H}}\right\}
.
Following
Examples

3.6
and
3.9
, the domain of definition of the composite is the subspace of
\ast
comprising the events whose corresponding distribution over
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{H,T}}\right\}
assigns the domain
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{H}}\right\}
of
f
probability
1
, and is thus in fact empty.
The corresponding substochastic kernel assigns probability
0
to
a
.

On the other hand, as subprobability measures
f
would instead assign probability
1
to heads and
0
to tails.
The composite would then be given by the Chapman-Kolmogorov equation, and in particular the composite would assign probability to
a
the probability

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}p}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a}}\right)=\sum_{x\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{H,T}}\right\}}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a\,|\,x}}\right)p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)=f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a\,|\,H}}\right)p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(H}}\right)+f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a\,|\,T}}\right)p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T}}\right)=1\cdot\frac{1}{2}+0\cdot\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{2}

The same applies to
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{SetMulti}}}}}\right)
and
\mathsf{Rel}
, where instead of
p
assigning probabilities
\frac{1}{2}
, we interpret
p
as having both heads and tails as possible outcomes.
Then the composite
f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}p
in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{SetMulti}}}}}\right)
would be defined only on the events such that
f
is defined on
all
possible outcomes of
p
, hence nowhere.
On the other hand the composite in
\mathsf{Rel}
would be defined on the events such that
f
is defined on
some
possible outcome of
p
, hence on all of
\ast
.

Remark 3.14
.

The common pattern in the comparisons of
3.13
is that the composition of two morphisms in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is defined on a “smaller domain” than in they would be in the categories it’s being compared to.
Indeed, as
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is entire and zerosumfree, the composition of two morphisms in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{BorelStoch}}}}\right)
(and
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{Dist}}}}}\right)
) will always have domain contained in that of the corresponding subprobability measure (the same holds for
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{SetMulti}}}}}\right)
and
\mathsf{Rel}
).
Considering the intuition that quasi-totality corresponds to determinism of domains, one may see this less intuitive composition as being required to ensure that the composites have deterministic domains.

Indeed, Di Lavore and Román have shown that the quasi-total morphisms of
\mathsf{BorelStoch}_{\leq 1}
and
\mathsf{Kl}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbf{D}_{\leq 1}}}\right)}
are the ones that assign each point of the source subprobability measures that are either probability measures or identically zero
[
DLR23
, Remark 2.18]
.
These are precisely the morphisms of those categories that correspond to ones of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{BorelStoch}}}}\right)
and
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{Dist}}}}}\right)
.
The counterexample of
3.13
thus also shows that composites of quasi-total morphisms in a (positive) CD category need not be quasi-total.
In particular, we cannot construct quasi-Markov categories by simply taking the quasi-total morphisms of an arbitrary CD category.
However, we show in
Corollary

3.19
that the partialization of a partializable Markov category is always quasi-Markov, and thus quasi-total morphisms are closed under composition in this class of category.

Remark 3.15
.

The composition law in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{SetMulti}}}}\right)
can be seen as a more “secure” version of the usual composition in
\mathsf{Rel}
.
Indeed, one could think of a relation as a nondeterministic program or process, which may fail (to terminate) on some inputs.
Then the typical composition in
\mathsf{Rel}
would track merely whether its possible to begin with a given input and reach a particular output.
However, there may be settings where failing to terminate on some possible input is catastrophic, and it would be preferable to only consider input output pairs such as all possible intermediate states lead to a defined output. That is, only allow inputs that ensure the composite process does not execute the first step and then catastrophically fail.
This is precisely what the composition in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{SetMulti}}}}\right)
achieves.

Remark 3.16
.

As remarked in
Section

2
, the hom-sets of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
are partially ordered with the partial order being witnessed by morphisms between the apices of the spans.
Specifically, for a parallel pair
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B,j,g}}\right)
represented by spans
X\xleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}Y
and
X\xleftarrow{j}B\xrightarrow{g}Y
, we have
(A,i,f)\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B,j,g}}\right)
if and only if there is a morphism
B\to A
filling in the following commutative diagram:
56
56

56

This morphisms is necessarily a deterministic monomorphism, as in
Lemma

A.2
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

(3.1)

This partial order features in all the works mentioned in
Remark

1.19
, where it is shown to be equivalent to the order of
Definition

2.10
in the cartesian case.
We show that this equivalence holds for
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
as well in
Corollary

3.20
.

3.3
Partializations are quasi-Markov

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
and the span category
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.
Cockett and Lack show that for any stable system of monics
\mathcal{M}
in a category
\mathsf{C}
, the category of spans
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C},\mathcal{M}}}}\right)
has a restriction structure given by
\overline{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)}\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,i}}\right)

[
CL02
, Proposition 3.1]
.
While this is defined independently of any monoidal structure, in the cartesian case it can be shown to be equivalent to the restriction structure associated to a
p
-category
[
CL02
, Examples 2.1.3 (6)]
, which is in particular a special case of that described in
Proposition

3.1
.

In light of
Proposition

3.1
, the domain construction defining a restriction structure may appear to be a rather strong condition.
Indeed, even the first condition “R.
1
” requires the category to be quasi-Markov, which already excludes plausible candidates for “partial stochastic map” categories such as
\mathsf{BorelStoch}_{\leq 1}
and
\mathsf{Kl}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbf{D}_{\leq 1}}}\right)}
(discussed in detail in
Remark

3.14
).
57
57

57

The category
\mathsf{Rel}
is in fact quasi-Markov, but fails to be a restriction category as the fourth condition “R.
4
” fails. In fact this domain structure doesn’t even define a poset-enrichment under the restriction partial order of
Definition

2.10
.

We show that
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
becomes a (symmetric) monoidal category upon defining the monoidal structure in terms of the one of
\mathsf{C}
in the obvious way such that both the domain inclusions and the restricted morphisms are composed in parallel.
The third condition in
Definition

3.4
ensures that the resulting span has again a deterministic monomorphism as its domain inclusion.
We thereby obtain a
monoidal restriction category
in the sense of Heunen and Lemay’s
[
HPL21
, Definition 4.2]
(see also
Remark

1.20
).
58
58

58

The author would like to thank Tomáš Gonda for this observation.
59
59

59

However,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is not a
tensor
restriction category
[
HPL21
, Section 5]
. In particular, the
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}}}}\right)
construction is not the same as the
\mathcal{S}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt}}}\right]
construction of
[
HPL21
, Definition 3.1]
. Indeed, in
[
HPL21
, Examples 4.3, 5.21]
it is noted that the category of sets and partial functions is a monoidal restriction category that is
not
a tensor restriction category.

This monoidal structure in fact makes
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
a positive quasi-Markov category, and the induced restriction structure as in
Proposition

3.1
is the same as that as the one defined in terms of spans (and hence the induced poset enrichment is the same as well).
This is shown in
Proposition

3.18
.

Proposition 3.17
.

We define a (bi)functor
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)\times\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)\xrightarrow{\otimes}\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
acting componentwise as the monoidal product of
\mathsf{C}
.
That is, we define

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\xleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}Y}}\right)\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{\prime}\xleftarrow{j}B\xrightarrow{g}Y^{\prime}}}\right)\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\otimes X^{\prime}\xleftarrow{i\otimes j}A\otimes B\xrightarrow{f\otimes g}Y\otimes Y^{\prime}}}\right)

This tensor product is well-defined, functorial, and is part of a symmetric monoidal structure extending that of
{\mathsf{C}}
.

Proof.

First note that the claimed product is well-defined, i.e. independent of the choice of representatives of the spans.
This is a consequence of the invertible morphisms being closed under tensoring, thus equivalent spans tensor to equivalent ones.

Moving to functoriality, the preservation of identities is immediate.
For the compatibility with composition consider morphisms
{\big(X\xleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}Y\big)}
,
{\big(Y\xleftarrow{j}B\xrightarrow{g}Z\big)}
,
{\big(X^{\prime}\xleftarrow{i^{\prime}}A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}\big)}
, and a fourth
{\big(Y^{\prime}\xleftarrow{j^{\prime}}B^{\prime}\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}}Z^{\prime}\big)}
.
The composites
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B,j,g}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B^{\prime},j^{\prime},g^{\prime}}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A^{\prime},i^{\prime},f^{\prime}}}\right)
can be computed by forming the pullbacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

after which the composites are represented by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(C,i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}u,g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}v}}\right)
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(C^{\prime},i^{\prime}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}u^{\prime},g^{\prime}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}v^{\prime}}}\right)
respectively.

The computation of the composite
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B\otimes B^{\prime},j\otimes j^{\prime},g\otimes g^{\prime}}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A\otimes A^{\prime},i\otimes i^{\prime},f\otimes f^{\prime}}}\right)
involves first forming the pullback of
f\otimes f^{\prime}
along
j\otimes j^{\prime}
. By
Proposition

A.12
we may as well use the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\otimes Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v\otimes v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\otimes u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\otimes j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f^{\prime}}

The composite is then computed to be
\bigl(C\otimes C^{\prime},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i\otimes i^{\prime}}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u\otimes u^{\prime}}}\right),\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(g\otimes g^{\prime}}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v\otimes v^{\prime}}}\right)\bigr)
, and functoriality follows.
Furthermore, the unit of
\mathsf{C}
is also the unit for this tensor product on
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.

The associators, unitors, and braiding of
\mathsf{C}
are additionally natural as maps of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.
For instance consider spans
\bigl(X_{1}\xleftarrow{i_{1}}A_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}Y_{1}\bigr)
,
\bigl(X_{2}\xleftarrow{i_{2}}A_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}Y_{2}\bigr)
, and
\bigl(X_{3}\xleftarrow{i_{3}}A_{3}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}Y_{3}\bigr)
.
Naturality of the associator
\alpha
with respect to these three morphisms of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C},\mathcal{M}}}}\right)
would mean

\alpha_{Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\Bigl(\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i_{1},f_{1}}}\right)\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i_{2},f_{2}}}\right)\bigr)\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i_{3},f_{3}}}\right)\Bigr)=\Bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i_{1},f_{1}}}\right)\otimes\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i_{2},f_{2}}}\right)\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i_{3},f_{3}}}\right)\bigr)\Bigr)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\alpha_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}.

(3.2)

The former is
\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{1}\otimes A_{2}}}\right)\otimes A_{3},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i_{1}\otimes i_{2}}}\right)\otimes i_{3},\alpha_{Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f_{1}\otimes f_{2}}}\right)\otimes f_{3}\bigr)
, while the latter can be computed using the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{1}\otimes A_{2}}}\right)\otimes A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{2}\otimes A_{3}}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1}\otimes X_{2}}}\right)\otimes X_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{2}\otimes X_{3}}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i_{1}\otimes i_{2}}}\right)\otimes i_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i_{2}\otimes i_{3}}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}}

The naturality of the associator with respect to the morphisms
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
,
f_{3}
of
\mathsf{C}
then implies that this choice of pullbacks constructs the same span, and consequently that
\alpha
is natural in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
as well.

Naturality for the unitors and braiding is a similar consequence of them being isomorphisms.
The coherence conditions hold because they hold in
{\mathsf{C}}
.
∎

The partialization construction thus preserves the symmetric monoidal structure (that is, the subcategory inclusion
\mathsf{C}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is a strong symmetric monoidal functor).
What is more,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
also inherits the copy–discard structure of
{\mathsf{C}}
via the inclusion of
\mathsf{C}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.
Concretely, the copy and discard morphisms are represented by spans

X\xleftarrow{\mathrm{id}}X\xrightarrow{\cop_{X}}X\otimes X\qquad\text{and}\qquad X\xleftarrow{\mathrm{id}}X\xrightarrow{\discard_{X}}I.

(3.3)

These make
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
into a CD category.

Proposition 3.18
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
and a map
u\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
represented by a span
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)
.
As a category of spans,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
has a restriction structure given by
\overline{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)}\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,i}}\right)
.
The restriction
\bar{u}
of
u
in terms of spans is the same as the domain
\mathrm{dom}(u)
of
u
in the sense of
Definition

2.8
.
In particular, the assignment
u\mapsto\mathrm{dom}(u)
defines a restriction structure on
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.
60
60

60

This is the restriction structure we would expect for a positive quasi-Markov category, as in
Proposition

3.1
. While we do not need positivity here as the restriction structure axioms hold by hypothesis, we will see in
Proposition

3.24
that
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is indeed positive.

Proof.

The composite
\discard_{Y}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}u
is represented by the span
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,\discard_{X}}}\right)
(here we use that
\mathsf{C}
is Markov).
In light of this, we compute the domain
\mathrm{dom}(u)

u
X
X

in terms of spans using the second pullback from
Corollary

A.5
by forming

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i\otimes D}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\cop_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes{}\discard_{D}}

This is (the class represented by) the span
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,i}}\right)
.
∎

Corollary 3.19
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
.
Every morphism of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is quasi-total, so that
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is a quasi-Markov category.

Proof.

As noted in the proof of
Proposition

3.1
, this is merely the fact that the restriction structure axiom “R.
1
” (recall
Definition

2.3
) for this restriction structure is the statement of quasi-totality (
Definition

2.9
).
∎

Corollary 3.20
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
.
The extension partial order
\sqsupseteq
(
Definition

2.10
) on the hom-sets of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
coincides with the partial order
\geq
defined in terms of spans (
Remark

3.16
).

Proof.

In light of
Proposition

3.18
, this is a consequence of the fact that the partial order in terms of spans and that in terms of domain idempotents are the partial orders induced by the same restriction structure.
That is, both partial orders are defined by
u\leq v
whenever
u=v\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\bar{u}
, where
\overline{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)}
is either
\mathrm{dom}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right))
or
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,i}}\right)
.
The former is the definition of
\sqsupseteq
(
Definition

2.10
), while the latter can be shown to be equivalent to the span definition of
\geq
(
Remark

3.16
).
The equivalence of the latter is standard in restriction category theory, but we include a proof for explicitness.

For this, we must show that for parallel spans
(D_{f},i,f),(D_{g},j,g)\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{f},i,f}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{g},j,g}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{f},i,i}}\right)
if and only if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Note that the composite
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{g},j,g}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{f},i,i}}\right)
is computed by forming the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Thus, if this is equal to
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{f},i,f}}\right)
, there must be an isomorphism
t\colon D_{f}\xrightarrow{\simeq}A
such that
i=i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}k\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}t
and
f=g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}t
.
61
61

61

This line of argument will return more generally in
Lemma

4.14
.

In particular,
h\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}t
is a morphism
D_{f}\to D_{g}
witnessing the span order of
Remark

3.16
.

On the other hand given a map
D_{f}\xrightarrow{t}D_{g}
such that
i=j\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}t
and
f=g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}t
, the pullback
A
computing
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{g},j,g}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{f},i,i}}\right)
can be computed as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

which by construction witnesses the equality
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{f},i,f}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{g},j,g}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{f},i,i}}\right)
.
∎

The original Markov category sits within its partialization and can be recognized in terms of spans whose domain inclusions are isomorphisms.
Alternatively, it also corresponds to the total morphisms in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.

Proposition 3.21
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
.
A morphism of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is total (commutes with deletion) if and only if it is (the inclusion of) a morphism from
\mathsf{C}
.
62
62

62

This is really about the total morphisms in the sense of
p
-categories or restriction categories being the same as the total morphisms in the sense of CD categories. In this sense it is also a corollary of
Proposition

3.18
.

Proof.

The backward direction is immediate.

For the converse, assume that
u
is a total morphism of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
represented by a span
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)
.
The condition
\discard\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}u=\discard
corresponds to the existence of an isomorphism that makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\discard}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\discard}

commute.
Commutativity of the triangle on the left is equivalently invertibility of the domain inclusion
i
, and hence
u
can equivalently be represented by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X,\mathrm{id},f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}i^{-1}}}\right)
, proving the claim.
∎

We can also identify copyable morphisms in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
as those in the partialization of the deterministic subcategory
\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{det}}
.

Proposition 3.22
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
.
A morphism
X\xrightarrow{u}Y
of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
represented by a span
\bigl(X\xleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}Y\bigr)
is copyable if and only if
f
is deterministic.

Proof.

The composite
\cop\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}u
is represented by the span
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,\cop\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f}}\right)
.
Using the third pullback from
Corollary

A.5
, the composite
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u\otimes u}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\cop
can be computed by forming the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\otimes Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\otimes i}

(3.4)

and is thus represented by the span
\bigl(X\xleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\otimes f}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\cop}Y\bigr)
.
Therefore,
u
is copyable if and only if
f
is copyable, which, in a Markov category, is equivalent to being deterministic.
∎

Example 3.23
.

For the partializable Markov category
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
, the copyable morphisms of its partialization are precisely the spans whose right way maps are deterministic, i.e. measurable maps.
Thus, copyable morphisms of type
X\to Y
are given by pairs
(S,f)
of a measurable subset
S
of
X
and a measurable map
f\colon S\to Y
.

3.4
Positivity

Proposition 3.24
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
.
Then,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is positive as well.

Proof.

We must show that for morphisms
u=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\xleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}Y}}\right),v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y\xleftarrow{j}B\xrightarrow{g}Z}}\right)
of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
such that
v\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}u
is copyable, we have

v
=
u
Z
Y
Z
Y
X
X
u
u
v

(3.5)

Form the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

So that

can be computed as
(X\xleftarrow{i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\iota}C\xrightarrow{g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h}Z)
.
As this is copyable, so is

(in
\mathsf{C}
, where copyability and determinism coincide).

The left morphism of

can be computed as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\otimes Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\otimes Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C\otimes\iota)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\cop_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\otimes i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\otimes Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\otimes Y}

On the other hand, the right morphism of
Equation

3.5
can be computed as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\otimes Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\otimes Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes j)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\cop_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\otimes Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\otimes Y}

Thus (as
f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\iota=j\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h
), the required equality
Equation

3.5
is immediate if it is shown that

g
j
=
h
Z
Y
Z
Y
C
C
h
h
g
j

This is a consequence of

g
=
h
Z
B
Z
B
C
C
h
h
g

which is the result of applying the positivity of
\mathsf{C}
to the deterministic
g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h
.
∎

4
Transfer of properties from a Markov category to its partialization

We now consider notions of use in categorical probability theory that can be transferred from a Markov category to its partialization.

4.1
Representability

Representability is a key property of many Markov categories of interest.
Intuitively, it lets us convert between random maps and deterministic maps that instead return the distribution of the random map’s output.
In a partially defined context, the intuition is the same, except only on the domain of definition of the random map.

Construction 4.1
.

Consider a representable and partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
with distribution functor
P
.
Then for fixed objects
X
and
Y
, we have a well defined map

\displaystyle\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X,Y}}\right)\to

\displaystyle\,\,\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X,PY}}\right)

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\xleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}Y}}\right)\mapsto

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\xleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f^{\sharp}}PY}}\right)

(4.2)

as a consequence of isomorphisms being deterministic, as then we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\implies}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime}}^{\sharp}}

As
f^{\sharp}
is deterministic it follows that this map actually factors to a map

\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)(X,Y)\to\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)_{\mathrm{cop}}(X,PY)

Proposition 4.2
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
that is additionally representable.
The sampling maps of
\mathsf{C}
include to morphisms of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
that define distribution objects, i.e. natural isomorphisms

\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)_{\mathrm{cop}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt},PY}}\right)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{samp}_{*}}\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.59998pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.50003pt]{6.00006pt}{0.50003pt}}{\kern 0.59998pt},Y}}\right)

for every object
Y
.
Consequently,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is representable.

Proof.

This argument is more or less a reduction to the bijection on the morphisms of
\mathsf{C}
combined with the fact that two spans with the same domain inclusion are equivalent if and only if they have the same “right way map”.

It suffices to check that the component at
X
is invertible for arbitrary
X
.
This map has a section given by
4.1
as for any map
f
of
\mathsf{C}
, we have
\mathsf{samp}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f^{\sharp}=f
.

However, this section is also a retraction.
Indeed, a copyable morphism
X\to PY
in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is represented by a span
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\xhookleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{g}PY}}\right)
with
g
deterministic.
For a
deterministic

A\xrightarrow{g}PY
, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{samp}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}g}}\right)^{\sharp}=g
.
Thus, applying the operation of
4.1
to
\mathsf{samp}_{*}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\xhookleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{g}PY}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\xhookleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{\mathsf{samp}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}g}Y}}\right)
returns
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\xhookleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{g}PY}}\right)
itself.
∎

Example 4.3
.

The quasi-Markov categories
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{BorelStoch}}}}\right)
,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{Dist}}}}\right)
(or more generally,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{Kl}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{R}}}\right)}}}}\right)
for
R
an entire zerosumfree semiring), and
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{SetMulti}}}}\right)
are all representable since the underlying partializable Markov categories are.
63
63

63

Representability of the Markov categories is a consequence of
[
FGPR23
, Example 3.2, Propositions 3.4 and 3.6]
.

On the other hand,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{FinStoch}}}}\right)
(like the underlying Markov category) is not representable, as the collection of probability measures on a finite set is not finite in general.

Lemma 4.4
.

Consider a deterministic monomorphism
i\colon A\rightarrowtail X
in a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
.
If
\mathsf{C}
is representable, the following naturality square is a pullback
64
64

64

The author would particularly like to thank Antonio Lorenzin for suggestions here.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{samp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{samp}}

Proof.

By the axioms of a partializable Markov category, there is a pullback in
\mathsf{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{samp}}

with
j
a deterministic monomorphism.

By the commutativity of the diagram,
\mathsf{samp}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}j=i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f
.
From representability, we also have
i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f=\mathsf{samp}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}Pi\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f^{\sharp}
.
Since both
j
and
Pi\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f^{\sharp}
are deterministic, and
\mathsf{samp}
induces a bijection between deterministic morphisms
B\to PX
and morphisms
B\to X
, we conclude that
Pi\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f^{\sharp}=j
.
In particular, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{samp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{samp}}

The fact that
Pi
is a monomorphism
65
65

65

P
, being a right adjoint, preserves monomorphisms.
shows that
f^{\sharp}
has a section given by applying the universal property of the pullback to the pair
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Pi,\mathsf{samp}}}\right)
66
66

66

As for such an induced map
s
, one would have
Pi\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f^{\sharp}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}s=j\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}s=Pi
.
.
However
f^{\sharp}
is itself monic as
j
is, and is consequently invertible. ∎

Proposition 4.5
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
.
The “pushforward”
P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)
of a morphism of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
represented by a span
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\xhookleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}Y}}\right)
can be computed as
PX\xhookleftarrow{Pi}PA\xrightarrow{Pf}PY
.

Proof.

First,
P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)
represents the left vertical morphism in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)_{\mathrm{cop}}(PX,PX)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)(PX,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)_{\mathrm{cop}}(PX,PY)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)(PX,Y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

By tracing
\mathrm{id}_{PX}
through the square,
P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)\colon PX\to PY
can be computed in terms of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{samp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

as
PX\xleftarrow{j}B\xrightarrow{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}g}}\right)^{\sharp}}PY
.
However, in light of
Lemma

4.4
such a pullback can be constructed as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{samp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{samp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

Consequently,
P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)
can be computed as
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(PA,Pi,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathsf{samp}}}\right)^{\sharp}}}\right)
.
The claim is now a consequence of the fact that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathsf{samp}}}\right)^{\sharp}=Pf
in
\mathsf{C}
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

Note that the unit and counit of the representability adjunction are total morphisms.
Thus, the adjunction
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)_{\mathrm{cop}}\rightleftarrows\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
restricts to the representability adjunction of
\mathsf{C}
,

\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{det}}\cong\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)_{\mathrm{det}}\rightleftarrows\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)_{\mathrm{tot}}\cong\mathsf{C}

4.1.1
Partial algebras

Consider a representable partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
with distribution monad
P
.
We have seen in
Proposition

4.2
that the unit
\delta
and counit
\mathsf{samp}
of the representability adjunction for
\mathsf{C}
include to define representability data for
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.

Thus in particular there is a distribution monad on
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)_{\mathrm{cop}}
, which acts the same as
P
on objects and has the same unit and multiplication maps.
