Proposition 4.7
.

An algebra for this monad on
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)_{\mathrm{cop}}
consists of an object
A
together with a partial morphism
PA\to A
, represented by a span in
{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathrm{det}}
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

such that in
\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{det}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\urcorner}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\urcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pd}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

(4.3)

Or in other words:

(i)

The unit map
\eta\colon A\to PA
factors (uniquely) through the domain map
d
into a section
s
of
a
;
67
67

67

In this case
\eta
and
d\colon D\to PA
necessarily have
A
as a pullback.

(ii)

The multiplication
\mu\colon P^{2}A\to PA
and the domain map
d\colon D\to PA
have the same pullback
D^{\prime}
as
Pa
and
d
;

(iii)

The diagrams of
Equation

4.3
commute.

We call an algebra for
P
on
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)_{\mathrm{cop}}
a
partial algebra
(in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
).

Intuitively this means:

(i)

The domain
D
of the partial algebra contains the
\delta
-distributions;

(ii)

The distributions on distributions whose means are in
D
are the random distributions on
D
for which the distribution of its “values” under
a
(which will typically be some sort of expectation) lies in
D
;

(iii)

The algebra acts on the mean of such a random distribution in a manner identical to its action on the pushforward of such a random distribution (under the algebra map itself).

Proof.

The two diagrams correspond to the unit triangle and associativity square of the algebra axioms respectively.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,d,a}}\right)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,d,a}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,d,a}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,d,a}}\right)}

The equivalence of the unit triangle with the first diagram is an instance of
Lemma

4.16
(with
s
being the section of the “right way map”
a
that
\delta
factors through).
This shows claim
(i)
.
So consider the associativity square, keeping in mind that
P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(PA\xleftarrow{d}D\xrightarrow{a}A}}\right)=P^{2}A\xleftarrow{Pd}PD\xrightarrow{Pa}PA
.
The composites
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,d,a}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mu
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,d,a}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(PD,Pd,Pa}}\right)
are represented by the spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pd}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

Thus, their equivalence implies the existence of an isomorphism
D_{1}\xrightarrow{\simeq}D_{2}
commuting with the composite span legs.
Thus we may replace
D_{1}
with
D_{2}
using this isomorphism, with
D_{2}
then playing the role of
D^{\prime}
in the statement of the proposition.
This is precisely claim
(ii)
.
Furthermore, once this identification is made (so that the span legs are equal on the nose, not just up to the isomorphism between the apices), the equality of span legs is precisely claim
(iii)
.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

In
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{BorelStoch}}}}}\right)
, the integration map gives
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the structure of a partial algebra (defined on the distributions where this integral is finite).
68
68

68

The author would like to thank Paolo Perrone for suggesting this line of example.

Proof.

Consider the following subset of
P\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
:

D\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p\in P\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\ :\ \int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}x\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)<\infty}}\right\}

For
p\in D
, set

r\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)\coloneqq\mathbf{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[p}}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}x\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)

We must show that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(P\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\hookleftarrow D\xrightarrow{r}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}}\right)
equips
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with a
P
-algebra structure.

Recall that the Giry
\sigma
-algebra
[
Gir82
, Section 1.2]
on
P\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is such that for all measurable
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, the assignment
\mathrm{ev}_{A}\colon P\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
p\mapsto p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A}}\right)
is measurable.
Consequently for any simple function
f=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\mathbf{I}_{A_{i}}
,
69
69

69

Where
\mathbf{I}_{A}
is the indicator function at
A
.
we have a measurable map

\displaystyle r_{f}\colon P\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle p\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{i}}}\right)

Now if
f_{n}\uparrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
is an approximation of the identity of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by simple functions, then we also have
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}f_{n}p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)\uparrow\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}x\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)
.
In particular, this monotone sequence converges if and only if it is bounded, so

D=\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p\ :\ \int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}f_{n}p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)<m}}\right\}

Here each innermost set
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p\ :\ \int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}f_{n}p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)<m}}\right\}=r_{f_{n}}^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\infty,m}}\right]
is measurable, so
D
is as well.
Furthermore, on
D
we deduce from
r_{f_{n}}\uparrow r
that
r
is measurable as well.

Thus
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(P\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\hookleftarrow D\xrightarrow{r}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}}\right)
is a span in
\mathsf{BorelMeas}\cong\mathsf{BorelStoch}_{\mathrm{det}}
.
We verify the conditions of
Proposition

4.7
.
For condition
(i)
, note that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}y\,\delta_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dy}}\right)=x
.
Thus,
\delta_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
factors across
D
through a section of
r
.

For condition
(ii)
, we compute the pullbacks in question

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{2}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pr}

As in
Example

3.6
, we may compute the pullbacks
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
explicitly as measurable subspaces of
P^{2}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
PD
respectively.
Explicitly,

\displaystyle C_{1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\pi\in P^{2}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\ :\ \mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi}}\right)\in D}}\right\}

\displaystyle C_{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\pi\in PD\ :\ r_{*}\pi\in D}}\right\}

The first is the
\pi\in P^{2}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

\int_{x\geq 0}x\,\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)=\int_{x\geq 0}x\int_{p}\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dp}}\right)\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)=\int_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{x\geq 0}x\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)}}\right)\,\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dp}}\right)<\infty

Note that a necessary condition for this to hold is that
\int_{x\geq 0}x\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)
is almost surely finite, or in other words that
\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(P^{2}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\setminus D}}\right)=0
.
Such a
\pi
can therefore be identified with a distribution on
D
.

It now therefore suffices to show that for
\pi\in PD
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mu}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}r_{*}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi}}\right)
.
Indeed, that would imply that
C_{1}=C_{2}
(as they are defined by finiteness of one and the other respectively), as well as demonstrate condition
(iii)
.
For this, we need only observe

\displaystyle r\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi}}\right)}}\right)=

\displaystyle\int_{x\geq 0}x\,\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)=\int_{x\geq 0}x\int_{p}p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)\,\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dp}}\right)=\int_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{x\geq 0}x\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)}}\right)\,\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dp}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{p}r\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)\,\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dp}}\right)=\int_{y\geq 0}y\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r_{*}\pi}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dy}}\right)=r\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r_{*}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi}}\right)}}\right)\qed

Warning 4.9
.

One might hope that the integration map, perhaps defined on the distributions with finite first moment would make
\mathbb{R}
a partial algebra as well.
That is, one might conjecture that the analogous span
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(P\mathbb{R}\hookleftarrow D^{\prime}\xrightarrow{r}\mathbb{R}}}\right)
, where
D^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p\in P\mathbb{R}\ :\ \int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)<\infty}}\right\}
(and
r\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)\coloneqq\mathbf{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[p}}\right]
) would satisfy the conditions of
Proposition

4.7
.
However, this is false.
70
70

70

We leave open the question of whether there is a canonical way to make
\mathbb{R}
a
strict
partial algebra.

This span does indeed define a map of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{BorelMeas}}}}\right)
, and even satisfies the unit triangle condition. But the multiplication square only commutes up to restriction of domain.
To be precise, we only have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PPX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

Here, the left composite is defined on those
\pi\in PD^{\prime}
such that
\int_{p}\int_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x}}\right|\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dp}}\right)<\infty
, while the right composite is defined on those where
\int_{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{x}x\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)}}\right|\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dp}}\right)<\infty
.
And indeed, the latter condition does not imply the former.

For instance, if
U_{n}
is the uniform distribution on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-n,n}}\right]
for each
n
, we may consider mixtures
\pi\coloneqq\sum_{n}c_{n}\delta_{U_{n}}\in PD^{\prime}
for weights
c_{n}
summing to
1
.
This satisfies the latter condition as each
\mathbf{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[U_{n}}}\right]=0
.
However
\int_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x}}\right|\,U_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)=n
, so
\int_{p}\int_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x}}\right|\,p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dx}}\right)\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(dp}}\right)=\sum_{n}nc_{n}
which need not be finite (for instance, when
c_{n}\propto n^{-2}
).

4.2
Conditionals

Conditionals also transfer naturally from a partializable Markov category to its partialization, and can in a sense be constructed on the “maximum possible domain”.

Proposition 4.10
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
with conditionals.
Given a morphism
u\colon A\to X\otimes Y
in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
represented by a span
\bigl(A\xhookleftarrow{i}D\xrightarrow{f}X\otimes Y\bigr)
, the conditional
u_{|X}\colon X\otimes A\to Y
exists and is represented by the span
\bigl(X\otimes A\xhookleftarrow{X\otimes i}X\otimes D\xrightarrow{f_{|X}}Y\bigr)
.
In particular,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
has conditionals.

Proof.

We compute the right side of
Equation

2.3
(with
u
substituted for
f
).
This can be done by forming the diagram of pullback squares
71
71

71

We rotate the diagram for ease of reading. The domain inclusions run vertically whereas the “right way maps” run horizontal.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\otimes D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes Y\otimes D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes X\otimes D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes Y\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes X\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\otimes i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\otimes Y)\otimes i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop\otimes D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\otimes X)\otimes i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes f_{|X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D\otimes i)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\cop}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}

Here the first square is a pullback by
Lemma

A.1
, the second by
Proposition

A.12
, and the rest by
Corollary

A.5
.

Thus, the domain of the right hand side can be identified with
i
.
Similarly, the “right way map” can be identified with the composite of the right side of
Equation

2.3
(with
f
as itself).
So the “right way map” is
f
, and thus the right hand side is the morphism
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)=u
as desired.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
with conditionals.
Then
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is a partial Markov category in the sense of Di Lavore and Román in
[
DLR23
, Definition 3.2]
(and the weaker sense of
[
DLRS
, Definition 3.1]
).

In particular,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{BorelStoch}}}}\right)
,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{FinStoch}}}}\right)
,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{Dist}}}}}\right)
, and
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\mathsf{SetMulti}}}}}\right)
are all partial Markov categories (although as mentioned in
3.13
, different from the examples of
[
DLR23
,
DLRS
]
).
72
72

72

Fritz noted in
[
Fri20
, Examples 11.6 and 11.7]
that
\mathsf{FinStoch}
and
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
have conditionals (although for the special case of states Cho and Jacobs had already pointed out in
[
CJ19
, Theorem 3.11]
that existence was a consequence of classical results on regular conditional probabilities
[
Fad85
]
). Di Lavore and Román remark in
[
DLR23
, Remark A.3]
that conditionals exist in
\mathsf{Dist}
, extending the familiar construction for states that appeared in Cho and Jacobs’
[
CJ19
, Example 3.6]
. Fritz and Klingler have demonstrated that the analogous construction produces conditionals in
\mathsf{SetMulti}
in
[
FK23
, Proposition 13]
(this is Proposition 16 in the arXiv version).

Remark 4.12
.

Another quasi-Markov category with conditionals (and hence a quasi-Markov partial Markov category) is the usual category of relations
\mathsf{Rel}
(or more generally, bicategories of relations
[
LRSS25
, Proposition 2.14]
).
However as remarked earlier (
Remark

3.3
),
\mathsf{Rel}
is not poset-enriched under the restriction partial order of
Definition

2.10
, and thus cannot be positive (
Proposition

2.12
).
In particular, unlike Markov categories
[
Fri20
, Lemma 11.24]
, quasi-Markov (and hence CD) categories with conditionals need not be positive!

On the other hand, a property equivalent to positivity for Markov categories is “deterministic marginal independence”
[
FGHL+23
, Definition 2.4 and Theorem 2.8]
.
For quasi-Markov categories, “copyable marginal independence” (the direct generalization of the aforementioned DMI) is still implied by positivity (the argument of
[
Fri20
, Proposition 12.4]
still applies).
However, it can also be shown to be a consequence of the existence of conditionals (and thus can no longer imply positivity, as it would hold for categories like
\mathsf{Rel}
).

Fortunately,
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is positive irrespective of conditionals.

4.3
Idempotent partial morphisms

We conclude this section with a characterization of idempotent morphisms in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
, and a characterization of those idempotent morphisms with properties of importance in categorical probability.
In particular, we are interested in splitting, balance, strength, and being static.

Proposition 4.13
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
.
A morphism
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\xhookleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}X}}\right)
is idempotent if and only if it can be represented by a span
X\xhookleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}e}X
for an idempotent
e
of
A
in
\mathsf{C}
.
In other words, the idempotent partial maps are those that act as idempotents on their domain.

We prove the statement by means of the following lemma.

Lemma 4.14
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
and morphisms
X\xhookleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{f}Y
,
Y\xhookleftarrow{j}B\xrightarrow{g}Y
.
Then,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B,j,g}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)
if and only if
f
factors through
j
via a
A\xrightarrow{h}B
in
\mathsf{C}
such that
f=j\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h=g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.14
.

The composite
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B,j,g}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)
can be computed by forming the following pullback.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

The resulting span is equivalent to
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)
if and only if there is an invertible
t\colon C\to A
such that
i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}t=i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}j^{\prime}
and
f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}t=g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f^{\prime}
.
In such a scenario,
j^{\prime}=t
is invertible, so we can equivalently replace
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(C,j^{\prime},f^{\prime}}}\right)
with a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

for some
h
.
Since any such commuting square is a pullback (
Lemma

A.1
), we see that the equation in the hypothesis is equivalent to the existence of a
A\xrightarrow{h}B
such that
f=j\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h
and
f=g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.13
.

Applying
Lemma

4.14
, we see that the idempotence of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,f}}\right)
is equivalent to the existence of a
A\xrightarrow{h}A
such that
f=i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h
and
f=f\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h
.
Given the first (and the monicity of
i
), the second equation merely expresses the idempotence of
h
.
∎

Recall now that an idempotent morphism
e\colon X\to X
in a category is
split
if it can be written in the form
e=s\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}r
for some
s\colon E\to X
and
r\colon X\to E
such that
r\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}s=\mathrm{id}_{E}
.

Remark 4.15

((i) of
[
CL02
, Lemma 2.2]
)
.

Every monomorphism in a restriction category is total.
In particular, every monomorphism of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is total, and therefore the inclusion of a (mono)morphism of
\mathsf{C}
.
In fact, this is true of any quasi-Markov category as has been demonstrated in the companion work on empirical sampling
[
FGL+25
, Lemma 2.6]
.

Consequently, if a map in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
has a section, the section is the inclusion of a map in
\mathsf{C}
.
In particular, if an idempotent in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
splits as a section–retraction pair, the section is the inclusion of a map in
\mathsf{C}
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Consider a morphism
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y\xhookleftarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{r}X}}\right)
in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
for a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
.
Then, a morphism
s\colon X\to Y
of
\mathsf{C}
includes to a section
73
73

73

By
Remark

4.15
, this describes all sections in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.
of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,r}}\right)
if and only if
s
factors through
i
via a section
s^{\prime}\colon X\to A
of
r\colon A\to X
.

Proof.

The composition
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,r}}\right)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X,\mathrm{id}_{X},s}}\right)
can be performed by forming the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Proposition 4.17
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
and an idempotent
m
of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
, represented by

for an idempotent

as in

.
The idempotent
m
splits if and only if

does.

If

splits as
s\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}r
for a section-retraction pair
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s,r}}\right)
in
\mathsf{C}
, the splitting of

is given by the section-retraction pair
\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y,\mathrm{id}_{Y},i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}s}}\right),\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,r}}\right)\big)
.

Proof.

If
e
splits as in the hypotheses, it is an immediate consequence of the fact that monomorphisms pull back along themselves to the identity that the proposed section-retraction pair do indeed form a splitting of

.

Conversely, if

splits, then as in

4.16
the splitting is given by a retraction
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,r}}\right)
and section

in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
such that

is a section of

in
\mathsf{C}
.
Then, the equality
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}e}}\right)=i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}s\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,r}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,i,i\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}s\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}r}}\right)
implies that

, as claimed.
∎

Example 4.22

\mathsf{C}

5
Kolmogorov products

Let us now turn to Kolmogorov products.
In contrast to the previous section, some aspects of the theory of Kolmogorov products change in the passage from the Markov (semicartesian) to the more general quasi-Markov setting.

In general, given a partializable Markov category
{\mathsf{C}}
, a Kolmogorov product in
{\mathsf{C}}
induces (a strict) one in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
(defined in the sense of
Definition

2.16
, see
Proposition

5.5
).
However as alluded to in
1.15
, an arbitrary family of maps
\bigl(X_{k}\to Y_{k}\bigr)_{k\in K}
in a quasi-Markov category such as
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
need not define a cone for the universal property of the (strict) Kolmogorov product to apply to.
In particular (when
\mathsf{C}
has
K
-sized Kolmogorov products), this means that the (strict) Kolmogorov product would not define a
functor

\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)^{K}\to\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
, merely an assignment on objects.
In order to recover this functoriality we define a lax version of Kolmogorov products.

To illustrate explicitly why this is necessary, consider partial maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Their tensor product has domain
D\otimes E
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\otimes E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\otimes B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\otimes j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes g}

If we now project onto the first component by marginalizing, we have the following diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\otimes E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\otimes j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

which, as a diagram in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
, only commutes
laxly
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\geq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(E,j,g}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

Indeed, the right path has domain
D\otimes E
, while the left path has the larger domain
D\otimes A
.
(This is part of the reason why
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
is not a Markov category: the discard maps are not natural.)

More generally now, let
K
be a set and consider a
K
-indexed tuple of partial maps
\big(X_{k}\xrightarrow{(D_{k},i_{k},f_{k})}Y_{k}\big)_{k\in K}
.
One can form the diagrams of finite tensor products
X^{F}
and
Y^{F}
and their marginalizations, but because of the reason above, the maps
f_{k}
do
not
form a natural transformation between these two diagrams, they only form a
lax
natural transformation.
These, in general, do
not
induce a morphism between the Kolmogorov products
X^{K}\to Y^{K}
by the usual universal property of
[
FR20
]
.
In order to have such a map, and so to make Kolmogorov products
functorial on arbitrary partial maps
by their universal property, we need to slightly extend the universal property.

We therefore define
lax Kolmogorov products
.
75
75

75

The author would like to thank Tobias Fritz for suggesting a lax approach.

It can then be shown (see below) that:

•

Kolmogorov products in
\mathsf{C}
include to strict Kolmogorov products in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
;

•

The inclusion of a Kolmogorov product in
\mathsf{C}
is also a lax Kolmogorov product in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
;

•

The map
X^{K}\to Y^{K}
induced by a family of morphisms
(X_{k}\to Y_{k})_{k\in K}
and the universal property of the lax Kolmogorov product is represented componentwise/leg-wise
76
76

76

That is, having domain the tensor of the domain inclusions in
\mathsf{C}
, and similarly with the “right way maps”.
by the corresponding induced maps in
\mathsf{C}
.

Recall the definition of lax cone, which we instantiate directly for our purposes.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
K
be a set, and let
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{k}}}\right)_{k\in K}
be a
K
-indexed tuple of objects in a quasi-Markov category
{\mathsf{C}}
.
Consider (again) the diagram
X^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.32771pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.28683pt]{3.27773pt}{0.28683pt}}{\kern 0.32771pt}}}\right)}\colon\mathsf{FinSub}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathsf{C}}
formed by finite products and marginalizations (as in
[
FR20
, Section 3]
).

A
lax cone
over the diagram
X^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.32771pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.28683pt]{3.27773pt}{0.28683pt}}{\kern 0.32771pt}}}\right)}
is an object
A
of
{\mathsf{C}}
together with arrows
f_{F}\colon A\to X^{F}
for all finite
F\subseteq K
, such that for all subsets
G\subseteq F\subseteq K
, the following diagram commutes laxly,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\geq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F,G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{G}}

where
\pi_{F,G}
denotes the functor action (marginalization) of
X^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.32771pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.28683pt]{3.27773pt}{0.28683pt}}{\kern 0.32771pt}}}\right)}
on the inclusion
G\subseteq F
.

Definition 5.2
.

In the setting of the definition above, a
lax infinite tensor product
is a lax cone
\big(X^{K}\xrightarrow{\pi_{F}}X^{F}\big)_{F\subseteq K\mathrm{finite}}
which is universal in the following sense: for any other lax cone
\big(A\xrightarrow{f_{F}}X^{F}\big)_{F\subseteq K\mathrm{finite}}
there is a greatest morphism
A\xrightarrow{g}X^{K}
such that
\pi_{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}g\leq f_{F}
for each finite
F\subseteq K
, and this lax limit is further preserved by tensoring with an arbitrary object
Y
.

We call such a lax infinite tensor product a
lax Kolmogorov product
when the projections
\pi_{F}
are deterministic.
77
77

77

It can be shown that in such a case, a cone with copyable components defines a copyable induced map.

Intuitively, this can be read as asserting that a compatible (up to possibly restricting the domain) family of partial kernels to the finite products corresponds to a kernel to the infinite product, defined on the greatest possible domain.

Remark 5.3
.

We are following here the convention in enriched or
2
-category (or higher) theory of “strictness” corresponding to literal equality, “strongness” corresponding to equality up to an invertible
2
-cell,
78
78

78

The prefix “pseudo” is often used for this notion in a
2
-categorical setting, but the distinction will be irrelevant for us as the invertible
2
-cells we consider will always be identities.
and “laxness” corresponding to equality up to a
2
-morphism.
Of course, as we only consider here enrichment in posets, the distinction between strictness and strongness vanishes.

In the case of
Markov
categories, the distinction between lax and strict vanishes completely, as there are no non-identity
2
-cells (the partial order of
Definition

2.10
collapses).
There is thus only the notion introduced by Fritz and Rischel in
[
FR20
]
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
and a diagram
79
79

79

Note that we do not require that the diagram
J
be that of finite tensor products and marginalizations.

\mathsf{FinSub}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{J}\mathsf{C}

for some set
K
.
Assume that the deterministic subobject posets
\mathsf{Sub}_{\mathrm{det}}(A)
of each object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
have
K
-sized meets.
If
J
has a limit in
\mathsf{C}
, then the inclusion of this limit cone defines both a lax and a strict limit in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
\lim J\xrightarrow{p_{F}}J_{F}
be the limit projections in
\mathsf{C}
.
We will also denote for an inclusion
F^{\prime}\subseteq F
of finite subsets of
K
, the induced map
J_{F,F^{\prime}}\colon J_{F}\to J_{F^{\prime}}
.
We also denote
\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\mathsf{FinSub}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)
.

Now consider a lax cone
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A\xrightarrow{u_{F}}J_{F}}}\right)_{F\in\mathcal{F}}
in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.
If the
u_{F}
are represented by
A\xleftarrow{i_{F}}B_{F}\xrightarrow{f_{F}}J_{F}
, then the lax commutation diagrams
J_{F,F^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}u_{F}\leq u_{F^{\prime}}
for
F^{\prime}\subseteq F\subseteq K
correspond to diagrams in
\mathsf{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{F,F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{F,F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{F^{\prime}}}

With the
t_{F,F^{\prime}}
’s deterministic monomorphisms uniquely determined by the triangles
i_{F^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}t_{F,F^{\prime}}=i_{F}
.
Consequently for

Proposition 5.5
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
admitting Kolmogorov products of size
K
.
Given a family of objects
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{k}}}\right)_{k\in K}
in
\mathsf{C}
, the inclusion of the Kolmogorov product projections into
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
define both a lax and a strict Kolmogorov product.

Proof.

Note first that for an arbitrary object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
, the deterministic subobject poset
\mathsf{Sub}_{\mathrm{det}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A}}\right)
has
K
-sized meets by
Proposition

A.9
.

Consequently for an arbitrary object
Y
, we can apply
Lemma

5.4
to the diagram

\mathsf{FinSub}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{Y\otimes X^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\kern 0.29439pt}\mathord{\rule[-0.25766pt]{2.94443pt}{0.25766pt}}{\kern 0.29439pt}}}\right)}}\mathsf{C}\colon F\mapsto Y\otimes X^{F}

which has by hypothesis limit
Y\otimes X^{K}
in
\mathsf{C}
.
This means that the Kolmogorov product
X^{K}
(and its projections) tensored with
Y
in
\mathsf{C}
include to both a lax and a strict limit cone.
Thus, the inclusion of the Kolmogorov product projections (the particular case where
Y
is the unit) and their tensor with an arbitrary object
Y
are both lax and strict limit cones.
In other words,
X^{K}
is both a lax and strict infinite product. To see that this is further both a lax and a strict Kolmogorov product it suffices to observe that the projections
X^{K}\to X^{F}
are deterministic by construction.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
with Kolmogorov products of size
K
.
Given a
K
-indexed family of morphisms
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u_{k}}}\right)_{k\in K}=\big(X_{k}\xleftarrow{i_{k}}A_{k}\xrightarrow{f_{k}}Y_{k}\big)_{k\in K}
of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
, the family of maps
\big(X^{K}\xrightarrow{\pi_{F}}X^{F}\xrightarrow{u^{F}}Y^{F}\big)_{F\subseteq K\text{ finite}}
defines a lax cone over the diagram of finite products
Y^{F}
and marginalizations.
Furthermore, the morphism
X^{K}\to Y^{K}
induced by the universal property of the lax Kolmogorov product is
\big(X^{K}\xleftarrow{i^{K}}A^{K}\xrightarrow{f^{K}}Y^{K}\big)
(that is, computed by the
K
-sized product componentwise).

Proof.

First, for an inclusion
F^{\prime}\subseteq F
of finite subsets of
K
, we can compute
\pi_{F,F^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}u^{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\pi_{F}
by forming

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F,F^{\prime}}f^{F}=f^{F^{\prime}}\pi_{F,F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{F}}

On the other hand,
u^{F^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\pi_{F^{\prime}}
is computed simply as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{F^{\prime}}}

Consequently, the universal factorization
s_{F,F^{\prime}}
in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{F,F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F,F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F,F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F^{\prime}}}

witnesses
\pi_{F,F^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}u^{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\pi_{F}\leq u^{F^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\pi_{F^{\prime}}
.
To see that such a factorization exists, note that the two outer paths
B_{F}\rightsquigarrow X^{F^{\prime}}
in the diagram commute by the construction of the two spans.
The compatibility of the finite products
i^{F},i^{F^{\prime}}
with marginalization then means that this translates to the given maps defining a cone over the cospan
\big(X^{K}\xrightarrow{\pi_{F^{\prime}}}X^{F^{\prime}}\xleftarrow{i^{F^{\prime}}}A^{F^{\prime}}\big)
.
Thus, the given family of maps defines a lax cone, and therefore corresponds to a map
X^{K}\to Y^{K}
.

Following the construction of this map in the proof of
Lemma

5.4
, this map is represented by a span
\big(X^{K}\xleftarrow{\iota}B\xrightarrow{g}Y^{K}\big)
, where
\iota
is the meet of the various
\iota_{F}
(with projections denoted
B\xrightarrow{t_{F}}B_{F}
), and the map
g
is the map induced by the universal property of the Kolmogorov product applied to the family of maps
\big(g_{F}\coloneqq B\xrightarrow{t_{F}}B_{F}\xrightarrow{f^{F}p_{F}}Y^{F}\big)_{F\subseteq K\text{ finite}}
.

From
Lemma

A.13
, we see that
X^{K}\xleftarrow{\iota_{F}}B_{F}\xrightarrow{p_{F}}A^{F}
can be computed as
K
-sized products
X^{K}\xleftarrow{\iota^{K}}B^{K}\xrightarrow{p^{K}}A^{F}
.
Here, when
k\in F
,
B_{k}=A_{k}
,
p_{k}
is the identity and
\iota_{k}=i_{k}
.
Otherwise,
B_{k}=X_{k}
,
p_{k}
is the deletion map and
\iota_{k}
is the identity.
Furthermore, from
Lemma

A.10
, we see that the meet
\iota
is then given by
A^{K}\xrightarrow{i^{K}}X^{K}
.
In other words, the induced map
X^{K}\to Y^{K}
has the claimed domain inclusion.

Using this presentation,
g_{F}
can be identified as the map
A^{K}\xrightarrow{\pi_{F}}A^{F}\xrightarrow{f^{F}}Y^{F}
. Thus the induced map
g
induced by the universal property of the product applied to the family of maps
g_{F}
is precisely
f^{K}
.
In other words, the induced map has the claimed “right way map” as well.
∎

To summarise, while we cannot in general construct infinite tensor products of morphisms in
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
by following the construction used for
\mathsf{C}
verbatim, we can still construct an infinite tensor product functor componentwise.
This infinite tensor product of morphisms is still a universal property induced map, only now arising from a lax universal property.

Remark 5.7
.

Consider a quasi-Markov category
\mathsf{C}
with
K
-sized
strict
Kolmogorov products and a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
.
One may attempt to define an infinite copy
f^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)}\colon X\to Y^{K}
, intuitively given by “
K
independent samples of
f
” by the universal property of the strict Kolmogorov product, as with infinite products in the Markov case.
For instance, when
\mathsf{C}
is representable and
f
is the sampling map, the infinite copy
\mathsf{samp}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)}
can be seen as encoding “iterated sampling”, which comes up in a variety of contexts such as de-Finetti’s theorem
[
FGP21
, Theorem 4.4]
(where exchangeable morphisms are characterized as those that can be given by iterated sampling from some prior), or a strengthening of representability called “observational representability” where one can intuitively distinguish distributions by iteratively sampling from them
[
MP22
]
.

Explicitly, one would define
f^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)}\colon X\to Y^{K}
to be the unique morphism whose finite projections
\pi_{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)}
are given by
f^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F}}\right)}\coloneqq f^{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathrm{id}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F}}\right)}
, where
\mathrm{id}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F}}\right)}
is the
|F|
-output morphism given by iterated copying.

One might expect this to produce similar problems as with infinite products, that is, that this family of morphisms might not satisfy the marginalization coherence that would be required in order to apply the universal property of the (strict) infinite product.
While this is a point that would have to be addressed in a general CD category, this family of maps in the quasi-Markov
\mathsf{C}
is indeed compatible with marginalization.
Repeated application (to arbitrary but finite degree) of
Equation

2.1
shows that the family of morphisms
\{f^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F}}\right)}\}
is compatible with marginalization (where we define
f^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\emptyset}}\right)}\coloneq\discard\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f
).

In fact, given
lax
Kolmogorov products we have further the equation
f^{(K)}=f^{K}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathrm{id}^{(K)}
, with the caveat that
f^{K}
is only the map produced by the
lax
universal property (and computed componentwise).

Proposition 5.8
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
.
Assume that
\mathsf{C}
has
K
-sized Kolmogorov products (so that
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
has them induced from
\mathsf{C}
).
Let
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)
represent a morphism of
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.
Then, the infinite copy
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)}
of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)
is represented by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)}}}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)}
be represented by a span
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(E,j,g}}\right)
.
We first identify the domain inclusions, i.e
j
with
i
.
By repeated application (to any arbitrary finite degree) of the copyability of domains (quasi-totality), the domains of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)}
coincide.
Thus, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,\discard{}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(E,j,\discard{}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}g}}\right)
and therefore
i
and
j
are equal as subobjects.
Consequently, there is a
h
such that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,h}}\right)
.
To understand
h
, for finite
F\subseteq K
we can compute

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,\pi_{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h}}\right)=\pi_{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,h}}\right)=\pi_{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f}}\right)^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F}}\right)}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D,i,f^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F}}\right)}}}\right)

Consequently
\pi_{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}h=f^{(F)}
for arbitrary finite
F
, and therefore
h=f^{(K)}
as claimed.
∎

Some results on partializable Markov categories

Remark A.3
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
.
As a consequence of
Lemma

A.2
, the category
\mathsf{Sub}_{\mathrm{det}}(X)
of deterministic subobjects (in
\mathsf{C}
) of an object
X
is the same as
\mathsf{Sub}(X)
with
X
considered as an object of
\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{det}}
. Both can further be equivalently identified as the subcategory of the slice
\mathsf{C}_{/X}
of the deterministic monomorphisms and deterministic monomorphisms between them (which is a full subcategory).

Thus, meets in
\mathsf{Sub}_{\mathrm{det}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X}}\right)
(when they exist) correspond to limits of diagrams of deterministic monomorphisms over
X
.
In particular, the limit projections are also deterministic monomorphisms.

Corollary A.5
.

Consider a partializable Markov category. For every deterministic monomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes i)\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\cop}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\otimes i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}

Corollary A.6
.

For
\mathsf{C}
a partializable Markov category, so is
\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{det}}
.
The inclusion
\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{det}}\to\mathsf{C}
induces an inclusion
\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{det}}}}}\right)\to\mathsf{Partial}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathsf{C}}}}\right)
.

Remark A.7
.

A commuting square in which either of the two parallel pairs of morphisms are isomorphisms is a pullback.

A.1
Kolmogorov products in partializable Markov categories

Proposition A.8
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
that has Kolmogorov products of size
K
.
Given a family of deterministic monomorphisms
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{t}\xrightarrow{i_{t}}Y_{t}}}\right)_{t\in K}
, the product
i^{K}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{t\in K}i_{t}
is a (deterministic) monomorphism.

Proof.

Consider
f,g
such that
i^{K}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f=i^{K}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}g
.
Then, for any finite
F\subseteq K
,

i^{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\pi_{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}i^{K}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f=\pi_{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}i^{K}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}g=i^{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\pi_{F}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}g

so that
\pi_{F}f=\pi_{F}g
since the finite product
i^{F}=\bigotimes_{t\in F}i_{t}
is monic.
As
F
was arbitrary, the universal property of the Kolmogorov product ensures
f=g
.
∎

Proposition A.9
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
with
K
sized Kolmogorov products.
For an object
B
of
\mathsf{C}
, the meet in
\mathsf{Sub}_{\mathrm{det}}(B)
of a family
A_{k}\xrightarrow{i_{k}}B
of deterministic monomorphisms over
B
exists, and is given by the pullback
\iota
in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cop}

(where we again use the notation
i^{K}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{k\in K}i_{k}
)

Proof.

The morphisms
\iota
and
j
are pullbacks of deterministic monomorphisms and hence themselves deterministic monomorphisms (so in particular
\iota\in\mathsf{Sub}_{\mathrm{det}}(B)
).
Now if
j_{k}
is the marginal of
j
on to
A_{k}
for some
k
, we have
i_{k}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}j_{k}=\iota
so that
\iota\leq i_{k}
for each
k
.

Conversely, given a subobject
C\xrightarrow{i}B
such that
i\leq i_{k}
for each
k
, there are
s_{k}\colon C\to A_{k}
such that
i_{k}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}s_{k}=i
(necessarily deterministic monomorphisms by
Lemma

A.2
).
Then, the morphism
s\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s_{k}}}\right)_{k\in K}
satisfies
i^{K}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}s=i^{(K)}
and therefore witnesses
i\leq\iota
by the universal property of the pullback.
Consequently,
\iota
is the infimum of the
i_{k}
’s, as claimed.
∎

Lemma A.10
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
and a family of deterministic monomorphisms
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{k}\xrightarrow{i_{k}}X_{k}}}\right)_{k\in K}
.
For finite
F\subseteq K
and arbitrary
t\in K
, let

B_{F,t}\coloneqq\begin{cases}A_{t}&\text{if }t\in F\\
X_{t}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and set
j_{F,t}\colon B_{F,t}\to X_{t}
to be
i_{t}
when
t\in F
and the identity otherwise.
Let
j_{F}^{K}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{k\in K}j_{F,k}
and use the convention
i^{K}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{k\in K}i_{k}
.
Then
i^{K}
is the meet in
\mathsf{Sub}_{\mathrm{det}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{K}}}\right)
of the
j_{F}^{K}
(ranging over all finite subsets
F
of
K
).

Proof.

First,
i^{K}
is a lower bound as for each finite subset
F
, there is a witness to
i^{K}\leq j_{F}^{K}
given by the map
A^{K}\to B_{F}^{K}
(where
B_{F}^{K}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{k\in K}B_{F,k}
) formed by tensoring
i_{k}
on the
k\notin F
and the identity on
k\in F
.

Conversely, consider an arbitrary deterministic monomorphism
C\xrightarrow{\iota}X^{K}
that is a lower bound for the
j_{F}^{K}
’s.
Consider witnesses for this, say
C\xrightarrow{u_{F}}B_{F}^{K}
such that
j_{F}^{K}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}u_{F}=\iota
.
Then, the marginal
v_{k}\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{k}}\right\}}}}\right)_{k}
of
u_{\{k\}}
on to
B_{{\{k\}},k}=A_{k}
is such that
i_{k}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}v_{k}
is the marginal
\iota_{k}
of
\iota
on to
X_{k}
.
As
\iota
and the
i_{k}
’s are deterministic, we have

\iota=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\iota_{k}}}\right)_{k\in K}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i_{k}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}v_{k}}}\right)_{k\in K}=i^{K}\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{k}}}\right)_{k\in K}

In particular
\iota\leq i^{K}
, and as
\iota
was an arbitrary lower bound of the
j_{F}^{K}
’s,
i^{K}
is the infimum, as claimed.
∎

Remark A.11
.

In light of
Remarks

A.3
and
A.4
, the
Propositions

A.8
,
A.9
and
A.10
are really statements about cartesian products, as they refer to pullbacks and subobjects in the subcategory of deterministic morphisms.
In any case, the proofs are essentially the same whether the products are considered as cartesian or Kolmogorov.

On the other hand, we will also have cause to consider pullbacks along morphisms that need not be deterministic.
Here, the difference from the cartesian case will have to be dealt with.

Proposition A.12
.

Consider a partializable Markov category with Kolmogorov products of size
K
(when
K
is finite, this holds for any partializable Markov category).

The tensor product of
|K|
pullbacks along deterministic monomorphisms is a pullback.
That is, given pullbacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{k}}

for each
k\in K
with all the
i_{k}
’s deterministic monomorphisms, the following square is a pullback as well (where for a family of objects (resp. morphisms)
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{k}}}\right)_{k\in K}
we use the shorthand
X^{K}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{k\in K}X_{k}
).

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{K}}

As

Lemma A.13
.

Consider a partializable Markov category
\mathsf{C}
with Kolmogorov products of size
K
.
Given a family
\big(A_{k}\xrightarrow{i_{k}}X_{k}\big)_{k\in K}
of deterministic monomorphisms in
\mathsf{C}
and a finite subset
F\subseteq K
, set for each
k\in K

B_{F,k}\coloneqq\begin{cases}A_{k}&\text{if }k\in F\\
X_{k}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and
B_{F}^{K}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{k\in K}B_{F,k}
.
Let
j_{F}^{K}\colon B_{F}^{K}\to X^{K}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{k\in K}j_{F,k}
, where
j_{F,k}\colon B_{F,k}\to X_{k}
is
i_{k}
for
k\in F
and identity otherwise.
Then, the following square is a pullback
84
84

84

This generalizes
Corollary

A.5
. As with
Corollary

A.5
, this can be shown by an appeal to
Proposition

A.4
. However, there is again the immediate direct argument that we present.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{F}^{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F}}

where
p_{F}
(like
\pi_{F}
) is given by the identity on components belonging to
F
and deletion elsewhere.

Proof.

For each finite
F\subseteq K
, there are pullbacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{F}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{K\setminus F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{K\setminus F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\discard}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\discard}

Up to permuting factors, the commutative square of the hypothesis is precisely the tensor of these two pullbacks, and is hence also a pullback by
Proposition

A.12
.
∎
